The effect of classical civilization, on the other hand, was not an unmixed good: for it served all too quickly to stereotype in old forms a spirit which had been freshly reborn, and it set up a servile principle in the arts which has in part been responsible for the wreck of both taste and craftsmanship.

The first builders of the Renaissance, in Italy, were not primarily architects; they were rather supreme artists in the minor crafts; and their chief failing was, perhaps, that they wished to stamp with their personal imprint all the thousand details of sculpture, painting, and carving which had hitherto been left to the humble craftsman. Presently, the technical knowledge of the outward treatment of a building became a touchstone to success; and a literal understanding of the products of antiquity took the place in lesser men of personal inspiration. The result was that architecture became more and more a thing of paper designs and exact archaeological measurements; the workman was condemned to carry out in a faithful, slavish way the details which the architect himself had acquired in similar fashion. So the architect ceased to be a master-builder working among comrades of wide experience and travel: he became a Renaissance gentleman who merely gave orders to his servants.

Victor Hugo said in *Notre Dame* that the printing-press destroyed architecture, which had hitherto been the stone record of mankind. The real misdemeanor of the printing-press, however, was not that it took literary values away from architecture, but that it caused architecture to derive its value from literature. With the Renaissance the great modern distinction between the literate and the illiterate extends even to building; the master mason who knew his stone and his workmen and his tools and the tradition of his art gave way to the architect who knew his Palladio and his Vignola and his Vitruvius. Architecture, instead of striving to leave the imprint of a happy spirit on the superficies of a building, became a mere matter of grammatical accuracy and pronunciation; and the seventeenth-century architects who revolted from this regime and created the baroque were at home only in the pleasure gardens and theaters of princes. For the common run of architects, particularly in the northern countries, the Five Orders became as unchallengeable as the eighty-one rules of Latin syntax. To build with a pointed arch was barbarous, to build with disregard for formal symmetry was barbarous, to permit the common workman to carry out his individual taste in carving was to risk vulgarity and pander to an obsolete sense of democracy. The classics had, it is true, united Europe anew in a catholic culture; but alas! it was only the leisured upper classes who could fully take possession of the new kingdom of the mind. The Five Orders remained firmly entrenched on one side, the "lower orders" on the other.

Hereafter, architecture lives by the book. First it is Palladio and Vignola; then it is Burlington and Chambers; then, after the middle of the eighteenth century, the brothers Adam and Stuart's *Antiquities of Athens*. Simpler works with detailed prescriptions for building in the fashionable mode made their way in the late seventeenth century among the smaller fry of carpenters and builders; and they were widely used in America, as a guide to taste and technique, right down to the middle of the nineteenth century. It was by means of the book that the architecture of the eighteenth century from St. Petersburg to Philadelphia seemed cast by a single mind. We call the mode Georgian because vast quantities of such building was done in England, as a result of the general commercial prosperity of that country; but it was common wherever European civilization had any fresh architectural effort to make, and if we call this style "colonial" in America it is not to mark any particular lapse or lack of distinction.
===Chinese===
文艺复兴是欧洲心灵朝向罗马与希腊文明之形式、朝向古典文化之意义的一次转向。就后者而言，它的冲动分明是解放性的：它把人的灵魂从一座受刑的囚室中解救出来——在那座囚室里，尘世生活的卑下满足与天堂的至福之间，没有任何可供调剂的兴致或活动。随着文艺复兴，那亦神亦兽的生灵重新变成了人。再者，正当基督教世界的天主教文化在异端与怀疑论的影响下分崩离析之时，古典著作给了欧洲受教育者一个共同的主题，使他们免于彻底的思想漂泊。然而另一方面，古典文明的效用并非全然是福：它过快地把一个新获新生的精神铸进旧形式里定型，并且在诸艺术中立起一条奴性的原则——趣味与工艺的双重毁败，一部分正要归咎于此。

文艺复兴在意大利的第一批建造者，首要身份并不是建筑师；他们毋宁是小工艺门类中的至高艺术家；而他们主要的过失，或许在于一心要把个人的印记加盖到雕塑、绘画与雕刻的千百个细节上——这些细节从前一直留给卑微的工匠。不久，关于建筑外部处理的技法知识成了成功的试金石；而在次一等的人物那里，对古代作品的刻板理解取代了个人灵感。结果是，建筑越来越成了一纸设计与精确考古测量的事情；工人被判定要以忠实而奴性的方式去实现种种细节，而建筑师本人也是以同样死板的方式把它们学来的。于是，建筑师不再是一位在见闻广博、游历四方的同伴中间干活的营造师：他变成了一个文艺复兴式的绅士，只会向仆人发号施令。

维克多·雨果在《巴黎圣母院》（Notre Dame）里说过，印刷机摧毁了建筑——建筑迄今为止一直是人类的石头记录。然而印刷机真正的罪过，不在于它夺走了建筑的文学价值，而在于它使建筑的价值派生自文学。随着文艺复兴，现代识字者与文盲之间的那道重大分界，竟一直延伸到了营造上；懂得石料、懂得工人、懂得工具、懂得本行传统的石匠师傅，让位于懂得帕拉第奥（Palladio）、维尼奥拉（Vignola）和维特鲁威（Vitruvius）的建筑师。建筑不再努力把一种欢畅精神的印记留在建筑物的表面，而是沦为纯粹关乎语法正确与发音的事情；十七世纪那些反叛这套体制、创造出巴洛克（Baroque）的建筑师，也只有在王公的游乐花园和剧场里才如鱼得水。对于寻常的建筑师，尤其在北方诸国，五大柱式（Five Orders）变得像拉丁句法的八十一条规则一样无可置疑。用尖拱营造是野蛮，不顾形式对称营造是野蛮，容许普通工匠在雕刻中贯彻个人的趣味，便有坠入粗鄙之虞，是向一种过时的民主意识献媚。古典著作确实在一个普世的文化里使欧洲重新联为一体；但是唉！只有有闲的上等阶级才能充分占有这个新的心灵王国。五大柱式牢牢盘踞在一侧，「下等阶级」则被撇在另一侧。

从此，建筑靠书本过活。起初是帕拉第奥与维尼奥拉；继而是伯林顿（Burlington）与钱伯斯（Chambers）；再往后，十八世纪中叶之后，便是亚当兄弟与斯图尔特（Stuart）的《雅典古迹》（Antiquities of Athens）。一些更简明的著作，就按时髦样式营造开出详细的方子，在十七世纪晚期打进了木匠与营造商中的下层辈；它们在美国被广泛用作趣味与技法的指南，一直用到十九世纪中叶。正是靠着书本，从圣彼得堡到费城的十八世纪建筑，看上去才像由同一种心智浇铸而成。我们把这股样式称作乔治式（Georgian），是因为英国商业全面繁荣，大量此类建筑在那里落成；但凡欧洲文明尚需付出任何新鲜的建筑努力之处，这种样式都很常见；而我们在美国称它为「殖民地式」（colonial），并不是要标示什么特别的退步或不足称道。
===Original===
It was left for Alexander Pope, who himself was a dutiful Augustan, to sum up the situation with classic precision to Lord Burlington, who had published Palladio's *Antiquities of Rome*:

> "You show us Rome was glorious, not profuse,
> And pompous buildings once were things of use.
> Yet shall, my lord, your just and noble rules
> Fill half the land with imitation fools;
> Who random drawings from your sheets shall take
> And of one beauty many blunders make."

These lines were a warning and a prophecy. The warning was timely; and the prophecy came true, except in those districts in which the carpenter continued to ply his craft without the overlordship of the architect.
===Chinese===
这番局面，最终要由亚历山大·蒲柏（Alexander Pope）来总结——他本人正是一位恪尽本分的奥古斯都派文人——他以古典式的精确，向刊行了帕拉第奥《罗马古迹》（Antiquities of Rome）的伯林顿勋爵（Lord Burlington）写道：

> 「你向我们表明：罗马的光荣在于庄重，而不在奢靡，
> 堂皇的建筑物昔日原是实用之物。
> 然而，大人，你那公正而高贵的规则
> 终将使半个国土挤满模仿的蠢材；
> 他们从你的书页里信手撷取图样，
> 把一种美摹成了重重谬误。」

这几行诗既是警告，又是预言。警告是及时的；预言也应验了——除非在那些木匠继续操持本行、而未受建筑师辖制的地区。

### III / 三
===Original===
The first effect of the Renaissance forms in America was not to destroy the vernacular but to perfect it; for it provided the carpenter-builder, whose distance from Europe kept him from profiting by the spirited work of his forbears, with a series of ornamental motifs. New England, under the influence of an idol-breaking Puritanism, had been singularly poor in decoration, as I have already observed: its modest architectural effects relied solely on mass, color, and a nice disposition of parts. In its decorative aspects medievalism had left but a trace in America: the carved grotesque heads on the face of the Van Cortlandt Mansion in New York, and the painted decorations in some of the older houses and barns among the Pennsylvania Dutch pretty well complete the tally.

Classical motifs served to fill the blank in provincial architecture. As long as the carpenter worked by himself, the classic influence was confined to little details like the fanlights, the moldings, the pillars of the portico, and so on. In the rural districts of New England, from Maine to Connecticut, and in certain parts of New York and New Jersey and Pennsylvania, the carpenter keeps on building in his solid, traditional manner down to the time that the jigsaw overwhelms a mechanically hypnotized age; and even through the jigsaw period in the older regions, the proportions and the plan remained close to tradition. The classical did not in fact supplant the vernacular until the last vestiges of the guild and the village-community had passed away, and the economic conditions appropriate to the Renaissance culture had made their appearance.

The dwelling house slowly became more habitable during this period: the skill in shipbuilding which every sheltered inlet gave evidence of was carried back into the home, and in the paneling of the walls and the general tidiness and compactness of the apartments, a shipshape order comes more and more to prevail. The plastered ceiling makes its appearance, and the papered wall; above all, white paint is introduced on the inside and outside of the house.

Besides giving more light, this innovation surely indicates that chimney flues had become more satisfactory. Paint was no doubt introduced to keep the torrid summer sun from charring the exposed clapboards; and white paint was used, despite the expense of white lead, for the reason that it accorded with the chaste effect which was inseparable in the eighteenth-century mind from classic precedent.

Indeed, the whiteness of our colonial architecture is an essential characteristic; it dazzled Dickens on his first visit to America, and made him think that all the houses had been built only yesterday. The esthetic reason for delighting in these white colonial farmhouses is simple: white and white alone fully reflects the surrounding lights; white and white alone gives a pure blue or lavender shadow against the sunlight. At dawn, a white house is pale pink and turquoise; at high noon it is clear yellow and lavender-blue; in a ripe sunset it is orange and purple; in short, except on a gray day it is anything but white. These old white houses, if they seem a little sudden and sharp in the landscape, are at least part of the sky: one finds them stretched on a slight rise above the highroad like a seagull with poised wings, or a cloud above the treetops. Were anything needed to make visible the deterioration of American life which the nineteenth century brought with it, the habit of painting both wood and brick gray should perhaps be sufficient.
===Chinese===
文艺复兴样式在美洲的第一重效用，不是摧毁本土语汇，而是使它臻于完善；因为它给木匠兼营造师提供了一系列装饰母题，而这些人与欧洲相隔遥远，无从受益于其先辈们虎虎有生气的作品。新英格兰在捣毁偶像的清教影响之下，装饰格外贫乏，如我前面已经指出的：它朴素的建筑效果全然倚仗体量、色彩与各部分的精当布置。就装饰而言，中世纪风格在美国只留下一点痕迹：纽约范科特兰特宅邸（Van Cortlandt Mansion）正面上雕刻的怪诞头像，连同宾夕法尼亚德裔（Pennsylvania Dutch）一些较老宅屋与谷仓里的彩绘装饰，差不多就数完了这份清单。

古典母题补上了外省建筑中的空白。只要木匠还独自做工，古典影响便只限于扇形窗（fanlight）、线脚、门廊的柱子之类的小细节。在新英格兰的乡间，从缅因到康涅狄格，以及在纽约、新泽西与宾夕法尼亚的某些地方，木匠以他那种坚实而传统的方式一直营造到线锯（jigsaw）征服一个被机器催眠的年代为止；而且即便在线锯时期，较古老地区的比例与平面也依旧贴近传统。事实上，古典风格并没有取代本土语汇；直到行会与村庄共同体的最后残余消逝，而与文艺复兴文化相称的经济条件出现之后，它才真正取而代之。

在这一时期，住宅慢慢变得更宜于居住：那门在每一处避风小港都留有明证的造船技艺，被带回了家中；在墙壁的镶板以及房间整体的整洁与紧凑上，一种船具般井然的秩序越来越占上风。抹灰天棚出现了，裱糊的墙纸也出现了；而最重要的是，白漆被引入了住宅的里里外外。

这一革新除了增加采光之外，必定还表明烟囱烟道已经变得更加可靠。刷漆无疑是为了不让酷热的夏日阳光把外露的鱼鳞板（clapboard）晒焦；而之所以用白漆，则尽管铅白（white lead）价钱昂贵，是因为它合乎十八世纪人心目中与古典先例须臾不可分的那种纯洁效果。

的确，我们殖民地式建筑的这种白色是一种本质特征；狄更斯（Dickens）初访美国时曾为之目眩，以为所有的房子都是昨天才盖起来的。人们喜爱这些白色的殖民地农舍，其审美理由很简单：只有白色、唯有白色，才能把四周的光充分反射出来；只有白色、唯有白色，才能在与阳光相接处给出纯粹的蓝色或薰衣草紫的阴影。黎明时分，白屋呈淡粉与青绿；日到中天，它是明黄与薰衣草蓝；熟透的落日里，它是橙与紫；总之，除了阴天，它独独不是白的。这些白色的老屋，纵使在风景里显得有点突兀扎眼，至少也是天空的一部分：人们会看见它们平铺在大路上方微微隆起的高地上，像一只稳住双翼的海鸥，又像树梢上头的一朵云。倘若还需要什么东西来让人看清十九世纪随身带给美国生活的退化，那么给木头与砖头一律刷成灰色的习惯，或许就足够了。

### IV / 四
===Original===
If the architecture of the early eighteenth century in America is a little prim and angular, if it never rises far above a sturdy provincialism, it is not without its own kind of interest; and Faneuil Hall, for example, is not the worst of Boston's buildings, though it is overshadowed by the great utilitarian hulks that line the streets about it. By studying the classical forms at one remove, the builders of the eighteenth century in America had the same kind of advantage that Wren had in England. Wren's "Renaissance" churches, with their boxlike naves and their series of superimposed orders for steeples, had no parallel, so far as I am aware, in Italy, and certainly had no likeness to anything that had been built in classic times: they were the products of a playful and original fancy, like the mermaid. Mere knowledge, mere imitation, would never have achieved Renaissance architecture; it was the very imperfection of the knowledge and discipleship that made it the appropriate shell of its age. Coming to America in handbooks and prints, chastely rendered, the models of antiquity were, down to the Revolution, followed just so far as they conveniently served. Instead of curbing invention, they gave it a more definite problem to work upon.

It was a happy accident that made the carpenter-builders and cabinet makers of America see their China, their Paris, their Rome through a distance, dimly. What those who admire the eighteenth century style do not, perhaps, see is that an accident cannot be recovered. However painstakingly we may cut the waistcoat, the stock, the knee-breeches of an eighteenth-century costume, it is now only a fancy dress: its "moment" in history is over. The same principle holds true for Georgian or colonial architecture, even more than it does for that of the seventeenth century; for one might, indeed, conceive of a breakdown in the transportation system or the credit system which would force a builder to rely for a while upon the products of his own region; whereas, while our civilization remains intact there are a hundred handbooks, measured drawings, and photographs which make a naive recovery of antiquity impossible.

Once we have genuinely appreciated the influence that created early colonial architecture, we see that it is irrecoverable: what we call a revival is really a second burial. All the king's horses and all the king's men have been hauling and tugging vigorously during the last fifty years to bring back the simple beauties and graces of the colonial dwelling, and the collectors' hunt for the products of the Salem, Newburyport and Philadelphia cabinetmakers is a long and merry one; but the only beneficent effect of this movement has been the preservation of a handful of antiquities, which would otherwise have been impiously torn down. What we have built in the colonial mode is all very well in its way: unfortunately, it bears the same relation to the work of the late seventeenth and early eighteenth centuries that the Woolworth Building bears to the cathedrals of the Middle Age, or the patriotism of the National Security League to the principles of Franklin and Jefferson. Photographic accuracy, neatly touched up—this is its capital virtue, and plainly, it has precious little to do with a living architecture. Like the ruined chapel in *The Pirates of Penzance*, our modern colonial houses are often attached to ancestral estates that were established—a year ago; and if their occupants are "descendants by purchase," what shall we say of their architects?
===Chinese===
倘若说美国十八世纪早期的建筑有几分拘谨、见棱见角，倘若说它从未远远超出一种坚实的乡土气，它却并非没有自己一路的趣味；譬如法尼尔厅，就算不上波士顿最差的建筑，虽然它已被四周沿街排列的一座座巨大的功利主义庞然大物所遮没。十八世纪美国的营造者是隔着一段距离研习古典形式的，这让他们享有与雷恩（Wren）在英国所享有的同一种优势。雷恩的那些「文艺复兴」教堂，带着匣子般的中殿和层层叠置的柱式尖塔，据我所知在意大利并无对应，自然也与古典时代建成的任何东西毫无相似：它们是一种顽皮而原创的幻想的产物，就像美人鱼。单单靠知识，单单靠模仿，永远成就不了文艺复兴建筑；恰恰是知识与师承的不完全，才使它成为自己时代的合适外壳。古代的范本经典雅的摹绘，以手册和版画的形式来到美洲，直到独立革命为止，人们只在它们便于派上用场之处加以遵从。它们非但没有束缚发明，反而给发明提出了一个更明确的问题去施展。

一桩幸运的偶然，使美国的木匠营造师与细木家具匠隔着重重距离、朦朦胧胧地看他们的中国、他们的巴黎、他们的罗马。那些仰慕十八世纪风格的人或许没有看到：偶然是无法复得的。无论我们多么煞费苦心地裁制十八世纪服装的马甲、颈饰和马裤，如今那也只是一件化装舞会的行头：它在历史上的那个「时刻」已经过去了。同一条原则对乔治式或殖民地式建筑同样成立，甚至比对十七世纪的建筑更加成立；因为人们固然可以设想交通体系或信贷体系的崩溃，会迫使营造者有一段时日只能依靠本地的物产；然而只要我们的文明还完整无损，就有上百种手册、实测图和照片，使对古代的天真复归无从谈起。

一旦我们真正领会了造就早期殖民地建筑的那种影响，我们便明白它是无可挽回的：我们所谓的复兴，其实是一场二次下葬。五十年来，国王所有的马匹、国王的所有人马，一直在起劲地又拖又拽，要把殖民地住宅的质朴之美与雅致唤回来；收藏家们追猎塞勒姆（Salem）、纽伯里波特（Newburyport）与费城的细木家具匠的作品，追得漫长而欢畅；但这场运动唯一有益的效果，是保全了寥寥几件古物——否则它们早已被大不敬地拆毁。我们以殖民地样式建造的东西，就其自身一类而言都还不错：不幸的是，它们之于十七世纪晚期与十八世纪早期的作品，恰如伍尔沃斯大厦（Woolworth Building）之于中世纪的大教堂，又如国家安全联盟（National Security League）的爱国主义之于富兰克林与杰斐逊的原则。照相般的精确，再加以细细润饰——这是它的头号美德；而显然，它同活生生的建筑几乎毫无干系。一如《彭赞斯的海盗》（The Pirates of Penzance）里那座残破的小教堂，我们现代的殖民地住宅往往连着一片祖传的庄园——那庄园设立于一年之前；倘若它们的住客都是「购置而来的后裔」，那么对他们的建筑师，我们又该说什么呢？

## VI The Imperial Façade / 帝国门面



### I / 一



===Original===
The decade between 1890 and 1900 saw the rise of a new period in American architecture. This period had, it is true, been dimly foreshadowed by the grandiose L’Enfant, but if the superficial forms resembled those of the early republic, and if the precedents of classic architecture again became a guide, the dawning age was neither a revival nor a continuation.

In the meanwhile, fresh influences had entered. The generation of students who had studied in the École des Beaux Arts after the Civil War was ready, at last, to follow the lone trail which Richard H. Hunt had blazed. Richardson’s most intimate disciples reacted against the stamp of his personality and sought a more neutral mode of expression, consecrated by established canons of good taste. On top of this, the introduction of steel-cage construction removed the necessity for solid masonry, and placed a premium upon the mask. The stage was set for a new act of the drama.

All these influences shaped the style of our architecture when it arose; but the condition that gave it a substantial base was the rise of a new order in America’s economic life. Up to this time, the chief industrial problem had been to improve the processes of mechanical production and to stake out new areas for exploitation. One may compare these economic advances to the separate sorties of an army operating on a wide front: any lone adventurer might take his courage in his hands and exploit an invention, or sink an oil well, if he could find it. By 1890 the frontier had closed; the major resources of the country were under the control of the monopolist; it became more important to consolidate gains than freshly to achieve them. Separate lines of railroads were welded into systems; separate steel plants and oil plants were wrought into trusts; and where monopoly did not rest upon a foundation of natural advantage, the “gentleman’s agreement” began its service as a useful substitute. The popular movements which sought to challenge the forces of this new regime﻿—the labor movement, socialism, populism﻿—had neither analyzed the situation with sufficient care nor attracted the adherence of the majority. The defeat of Henry George as a local political candidate was symbolic: by 1888 a humane thinker like Edward Bellamy had already accepted the defeat, had embraced the idea of the trust, and had conceived a comprehensive utopia on the basis of letting the process of monopoly go the limit, so that finally, by a mere yank of the levers, the vast economic organizations of the country would become the “property” of the people.

===Chinese===
1890 到 1900 年之间的十年，见证了美国建筑一个新时期的兴起。诚然，好大喜功的朗方（L'Enfant）曾依稀预示过这一时期；但纵使表面形式与早期共和国的如出一辙，纵使古典建筑的先例再度成为指南，这个破晓中的时代既不是一场复兴，也不是一次延续。

与此同时，种种新的影响已然进入。内战后赴巴黎美术学院（École des Beaux Arts）求学的那一代学生，终于准备好踏上理查德·亨特（Richard H. Hunt）独自开辟的路径。理查森（Richardson）最亲近的门徒起而反抗他个性的烙印，寻求一种更为中性的表达方式，一种经既定雅趣准则认可而奉为正统的方式。加之钢框架结构的引入免除了实心砌体的必要，令面具身价大涨。这出戏剧的新一幕已然布置停当。

当我们的建筑风格兴起之时，所有这些影响都塑造了它；但赋予它坚实基础的，却是美国经济生活中一种新秩序的崛起。直到此时，工业的头号问题一直是改进机械生产的工艺、圈定可供开发的新区域。不妨把这些经济上的进展，比作一支在开阔战线上作战的军队所进行的一次次出击：任何一个孤胆冒险家，只要有胆有识，都可以去开发一项发明，或者钻一口油井——只要他找得到。到 1890 年，边疆已经关闭；国家的主要资源已置于垄断者的控制之下；巩固既得的利益，变得比重新猎取利益更为重要。各自分散的铁路线被焊接成系统；各自分散的钢铁厂和炼油厂被锻造成托拉斯；而在垄断无法立足于天然优势之上的地方，「君子协定」便开始作为一种有用的替代品效力。那些试图挑战这一新政权势力的群众运动——劳工运动、社会主义、平民主义——既未能足够细致地分析局势，也未能赢得多数人的归附。亨利·乔治（Henry George）作为地方政治候选人竞选失利，此中自有象征意味：到 1888 年，连爱德华·贝拉米（Edward Bellamy）这样富于人道情怀的思想家也已接受了失败，拥抱了托拉斯的观念，并构想出一个包罗万象的乌托邦，其基础是任凭垄断进程走向极限，以便最终只消猛地一扳操纵杆，全国庞大的经济组织便会成为人民的「财产」。

===Original===
The drift to the open lands came to a full pause. The land-empire had been conquered, and its overlords were waxing in power and riches: the name “millionaire” became the patent of America’s new nobility. With the shift from industry to finance went a shift from the producing towns to the spending towns: architecture came to dwell in the stock exchanges, the banks, the shops, and the clubs of the metropolis; if it sought the countryside at all, it established itself in the villas that were newly laid out on hill and shore in the neighborhood of the great cities. The keys to this period are opulence and magnitude: “money to burn.”

These years witnessed what the Roman historian, Ferrero, has called a “*véritable recommencement d’histoire*.” In the new centers of privilege there arose a scale of living and a mode of architecture which, with all its attendant miseries, depletions, and exploitations, recalled the Rome of the first and second centuries after Christ. It is needless to say that vast acres of buildings, factories, shops, homes, were erected which had no relation at all to the imperial regime; for not everyone participated in either the benefits or the depressions that attended the growth of monopoly; but the accent of this period, the dominant note, was an imperial one. While the commonplace building of the time cannot be ignored, it remains, so to say, out of the picture.

===Chinese===
涌向开阔地带的人潮完全止息了。土地帝国已被征服，其霸主们的权势与财富蒸蒸日上：「百万富翁」之名成了美国新贵阶层的专利。随着产业让位于金融，生产城镇也让位于消费城镇：建筑住进了大都会的证券交易所、银行、商店和俱乐部；它若还眷顾乡野，也不过是栖身于大城市近郊山巅海滨新辟的别墅里。这个时期的基调是豪奢与宏大：「烧不完的钱」。

这些年月见证了罗马史家费雷罗（Ferrero）所谓的「历史的真正重新开端」（*véritable recommencement d'histoire*）。在新的特权中心，兴起了一种生活排场和一种建筑样式，连同随之而来的一切苦难、耗竭与剥削，令人想起公元后第一、二世纪的罗马。自不待言，还有大片大片的建筑、工厂、商店、住宅兴建起来，与帝国体制毫无瓜葛；因为并非人人都分沾了垄断增长所带来的利益或萧条；但这个时期的重音、它的主导音调，却是帝国的。时下平凡的建筑固然不容忽视，但可以说，它依旧留在画面之外。
### II / 二



===Original===
Hardly had the process of concentration and consolidation begun before the proper form manifested itself. The occasion for its appearance was the World’s Columbian Exposition, opened in 1893. In creating this fair, the enterprise and capacity for organization which the architects of Chicago had applied to the construction of the skyscraper transformed the unkempt wilderness of Jackson Park into the Great White City in the space of two short years. Here the architects of the country, particularly of New York and Chicago, appeared for the first time as a united profession, or, to speak more accurately, as a college. Led by the New Yorkers, who had come more decisively under European influence, they brought to this exposition the combination of skill and taste in all the departments of the work that had, two centuries earlier, created the magnificent formalities of Versailles. There was unity of plan in the grouping of the main buildings about the lagoon; there was unity of tone and color in the gleaming white façades; there was unity of effect in the use of classic orders and classic forms of decoration. Lacking any genuine unity of ideas and purposes﻿—for Root had initially conceived of a variegated oriental setting﻿—the architects of the exposition had achieved the effects of unity by subordinating their work to an established precedent. They chanted a Roman litany above the Babel of individual styles. It was a capital triumph of the academic imagination. If these main buildings were architecture, America had never seen so much of it at one time before. Even that belated Greco-Puritan, Mr. Charles Eliot Norton, was warm in praise.

It would be foolish to quarrel with the style that was chosen for these exposition buildings, or to deny its propriety. Messrs. McKim, White, Hunt, and Burnham divined that they were fated to serve Renaissance despots and emperors with more than Roman power, and unerringly they chose the proper form for their activities. Whereas Rome had cast its spell over the architects of the early Renaissance because they wished once more to enter into its life, the life of its sages and poets and artists, it attracted the architects of the White City because of its external features﻿—because of its stereotyped canons and rules﻿—because of the relatively small number of choices it offered for a lapse in taste﻿—because of its skill in conspicuous waste, and because of that very noncommittal quality in its massive forms which permitted the basilica to become a church, or the temple to become a modern bank.

Of all the Renaissance architects, their impulses and interests were nearest, perhaps, to Robert Adam, whose church at West Wycombe could be turned into a ballroom by the simple act of removing the pews, and permitting the gay walls and decorations to speak for themselves. Behind the white staff façade of the World’s Fair buildings was the steel and glass structure of the engineer: the building spoke one language and the “architecture” another. If the coming of the skyscraper had turned masonry into veneer, here was a mode of architecture which was little but veneer.

===Chinese===
集中与合并的进程方才开始，合适的形式便自行显形。它登场的契机，是 1893 年开幕的哥伦比亚世界博览会（World's Columbian Exposition）。在创办这届博览会的过程中，芝加哥的建筑师们把建造摩天楼时的进取精神与组织才干拿了出来，短短两年之内，便把杰克逊公园（Jackson Park）杂乱荒芜的野地变成了伟大的白色城市。在这里，全国的建筑师，尤其是纽约和芝加哥的建筑师，第一次以一个团结的行业亮相，或者更准确地说，以一个学院亮相。在受欧洲影响更见决然的纽约人带领下，他们为这届博览会带来了技艺与品味在工程各部门的结合，而正是这种结合，在两个世纪之前造就了凡尔赛宫富丽堂皇的仪典排场。主要建筑群环绕潟湖布局，规划上有统一；熠熠生辉的白色立面，色调与色彩上有统一；古典柱式与古典装饰形式的运用，效果上有统一。由于缺乏思想与目的上的任何真正统一——因为鲁特（Root）最初构想的是一个斑斓的东方场景——博览会的建筑师们便让作品臣服于一个既定先例，从而取得了统一的表象。他们在个人风格的巴别塔喧嚣之上，齐声诵念罗马的连祷文。这是学院派想象力的一场登峰造极的胜利。如果说这些主体建筑算是建筑，美国还从未一次性见过这么多建筑。就连那位迟来的希腊式清教徒查尔斯·艾略特·诺顿（Charles Eliot Norton）先生，也赞不绝口。

要为这些博览会建筑所选的风格鸣不平，或者否认其得体，那是愚蠢的。麦金（McKim）、怀特（White）、亨特、伯纳姆（Burnham）诸位先生凭直觉便知道，自己命中注定要为权势更甚于罗马的文艺复兴式僭主与帝王效劳，于是他们毫厘不爽地为自己的活动选定了恰当的形式。罗马之吸引早期文艺复兴的建筑师，是因为他们渴望再度进入罗马的生活——罗马的圣哲、诗人与艺术家的生活；而它吸引白色城市的建筑师，却是因为它的外在特征——因为它那刻板的准则与规范——因为它留给趣味失足的选择余地相对之少——因为它在炫耀性浪费上的娴熟技艺，还因为它那庞大形式中恰不含立场的品质：正是这种品质，允许巴西利卡变成教堂，或让神庙变成一家现代银行。

在历代文艺复兴建筑师中，他们的冲动与趣味或许最接近罗伯特·亚当（Robert Adam）：他在西威科姆（West Wycombe）建的那座教堂，只需把长凳一撤，让轻快的墙面与装饰自己开口，便能改成舞厅。世博会建筑的白色灰泥立面背后，是工程师的钢与玻璃结构：建筑说着一种语言，「建筑」说着另一种。如果说摩天楼的到来已把砌体变成了贴面，这里便是一种几乎只剩贴面的建筑样式。

===Original===
In their place, at the Fair, these classic buildings were all that could be demanded: Mr. Geoffrey Scott’s defense of the Baroque, in *The Architecture of Humanism*, applies particularly to its essential manifestations in the Garden and the Theater﻿—and why not in the Fair? Form and function, ornament and design, have no inherent relation, one with the other, when the mood of the architect is merely playful: there is no use in discussing the anatomy of architecture when its only aim is fancy dress. As a mask, as a caprice, the classic orders are as justifiable as the icing on a birthday cake: they divert the eye without damaging the structure that they conceal. Unfortunately, the architecture of the Renaissance has a tendency to imitate the haughty queen who advised the commons to eat cake. Logically, it demands that a Wall Street clerk shall live like a Lombardy prince, that a factory should be subordinated to esthetic contemplation; and since these things are impossible, it permits “mere building” to become illiterate and vulgar below the standards of the most debased vernacular. Correct in proportion, elegant in detail, courteous in relation to each other, the buildings of the World’s Fair were, nevertheless, only the simulacra of a living architecture: they were the concentrated expression of an age which sought to produce “values” rather than goods. In comparison with this new style, the romanticism of the Victorian Age, with its avid respect for the medieval building traditions, was honesty and dignity itself.

The Roman precedent, modified by the work of Louis XIV and Napoleon III, by Le Nôtre and Haussmann, formed the basis not merely for the World’s Fair, but for the host of city plans that were produced in the two decades that followed. It seemed for a while as if the architect might take the place of the engineer as city planner, and that the mangled regularity of the engineer’s gridiron plan, laid down without respect to topographic advantage or to use, might be definitely supplanted in the remodeled central districts and in the new extensions and suburbs of the American city. The evil of the World’s Fair triumph was that it suggested to the civic enthusiast that every city might become a fair: it introduced the notion of the City Beautiful as a sort of municipal cosmetic, and reduced the work of the architect to that of putting a pleasing front upon the scrappy building, upon the monotonous streets and the mean houses, that characterized vast areas in the newer and larger cities.

If the engineer who had devoted himself to sewers and street-plans alone had been superficial, the architectural city planner who centered attention upon parkways alone, grand avenues alone, and squares like the Place de l’Etoile alone, was equally superficial. The civic center and the parkway represented the better and more constructive side of this effort: in Cleveland, in Pittsburgh, in Springfield, Mass., harmonious groups of white buildings raised their heads above the tangle of commercial traffic, and in the restoration of L’Enfant’s plan for Washington, the realities of the imperial regime at length caught up with the dreamer born out of his due time. A good many of these plans, however, were pathetically immature. One of the reports for Manhattan, for example, devoted pages and pages to showing the improvement that would follow the demolition of the wall around Central Park﻿—and the importance of clipped trees in the design of grand avenues!

===Chinese===
就地而论，这些古典建筑在博览会现场无可指摘：杰弗里·斯科特（Geoffrey Scott）先生在《人文主义的建筑学》（*The Architecture of Humanism*）中为巴洛克所作的辩护，特别适用于巴洛克在花园与剧场中的本质显现——为何不适用于博览会呢？当建筑师的心境纯属游戏时，形式与功能、装饰与设计，彼此之间并无内在关联：当建筑唯一的目标是化装舞服时，谈论建筑的解剖学毫无用处。作为面具、作为一时的奇想，古典柱式之正当，恰如生日蛋糕上的糖霜：它们悦人眼目，又不损伤其遮掩的结构。不幸的是，文艺复兴的建筑有一种癖好，喜欢效法那位劝平民吃蛋糕的傲慢王后。按逻辑，它要求华尔街的职员像伦巴第的君侯一样生活，要求工厂从属于审美的静观；而既然这些都不可能，它便听任「单纯建筑」堕入文盲式的粗俗，连最堕落的乡土标准都不及。比例正确，细部优雅，彼此礼数周全，世博会的这些建筑终究只是一具活建筑的拟象：它们是一个时代的集中表达，而这个时代追求的是生产「价值」而非商品。与这种新风格相比，维多利亚时代的浪漫主义——连同它对中世纪营造传统的热切敬意——简直是诚实与尊严本身。

这一罗马先例，经过路易十四与拿破仑三世的经营，经过勒诺特（Le Nôtre）与奥斯曼（Haussmann）的加工，不仅为世博会奠定了基础，也为随后二十年间产出的一大批城市规划奠定了基础。有一阵子，建筑师似乎要取代工程师充任城市规划者，而工程师那种罔顾地形之利与实际用途、被弄得残缺不全的整齐网格规划，似乎将在美国城市的改造中心区、新拓展区与郊区中被彻底取代。世博会胜利的祸害在于，它令市政热心人士以为每座城市都可以变成一场博览会：它把「城市美化」（City Beautiful）的观念引入为一种市政化妆品，把建筑师的工作贬低为给零碎杂乱的建筑、单调的街道和寒伧的住宅装点一个悦目的门面——而这三者正是新兴大城市里大片地区的特征。

如果说只管下水道和街道规划的工程师是肤浅的，那么只把注意力集中在林园大道上、只集中在宏伟大街上、只集中在星形广场（Place de l'Étoile）一类广场上的建筑规划师，也同样肤浅。市政中心与林园大道代表了这一努力中较好、较有建设性的一面：在克利夫兰，在匹兹堡，在马萨诸塞州的斯普林菲尔德，成组的白色建筑和谐地从商业交通的乱丛中探出头来；而在朗方华盛顿规划的复兴中，帝国体制的现实终于追上了这位生不逢辰的梦想家。然而，这批规划中有许多幼稚得可怜。譬如，一份关于曼哈顿的报告，竟连篇累牍地论证拆除中央公园四周围墙之后将出现的种种改善——还有修剪成形的树木在宏伟大街设计中的重要性！
===Original===
Plainly, the architect did not face with sufficient realism the colossal task with which he was confronted in the renovation of the city. He accepted his improvements too much at the value placed upon them by the leaders of Big Business﻿—as a creator of land-values, as an element in increasing the commercial attractiveness of the city. Did not Mr. Daniel Burnham himself point to the improvements in Periclean Athens, not as the embodiment of Athenian citizenship and religion at its highest point, but as a measure for increasing the attractiveness of the city to visitors from abroad? Cut off from his true function to serve and beautify the community, made an accessory of business itself, like the merest salesman or advertising agent, it is no wonder that the architect speedily lost his leadership; and that the initiative went once again into the hands of the engineer.

The main merit of all these efforts to perpetuate the World’s Fair is that they sought to achieve some of the dignity and decisiveness of the formal plan. Their weakness was that they neglected new elements, like the billboard, the skysign, the subway, the tall building, which undermined the effects of the plan even when it was achieved. In their efforts to escape from the welter of misguided commercial enterprise, the advocates of the city beautiful placed too great reliance upon spots of outward order and decency; they took refuge in the paper symmetry of axial avenues and round-points, as one finds them in Haussmann’s Paris, and neglected the deeper and more genuine beauties of, let us say, the High Street in Oxford or Chipping Camden, or of many another European town that had achieved completion in its essentials before the nineteenth century.

In short, the advocates of the city beautiful sought a remedy on paper which could be purchased only by a thorough reorganization of the community’s life. If all this applies to the better side of the World’s Fair, it touches even more emphatically the worse.

===Chinese===
显然，建筑师面对城市更新这一庞大任务时，拿不出足够的现实主义。他太轻易地按照大企业领袖们估定的价值来接受自己的改良——把自己当作地价的创造者，当作增进城市商业吸引力的一个要素。丹尼尔·伯纳姆先生本人不就是把伯里克利时代雅典的种种改善，说成不是雅典公民精神与宗教在极盛时的体现，而是招徕外国游客、增进城市魅力的一项举措吗？建筑师服务社区、美化社区的真正职能被割断了，沦为商业本身的附件，与最普通的推销员或广告经纪无异，无怪乎他迅速丧失了领导权；主动权再度落入工程师之手。

所有这些延续世博会的努力，其主要优点在于追求正式规划的某种庄重与果断。其弱点在于忽视了新的元素——广告牌、高楼招牌、地铁、高层建筑——即便规划实现之后，这些元素也在侵蚀规划的效果。城市美化的倡导者们为了逃脱误入歧途的商业企业搅起的混乱漩涡，过分依赖外在秩序与体面的零星点缀；他们躲进轴向大道与环形广场的纸面对称之中，一如人们在奥斯曼的巴黎所见，却忽略了更深层、更真实的美——比如牛津的高街（High Street）、奇平卡姆登（Chipping Camden），或任何一座在十九世纪之前便已在本质上臻于完成的欧洲小镇。

简言之，城市美化的倡导者们寻求一剂纸上的药方，而这药方唯有以社区生活的彻底重组才能购得。如果说这一切适用于世博会较好的一面，那么它更触目地适用于其较坏的一面。

===Original===
The twenty years between 1890 and 1910 saw the complete rehabilitation of the Roman mode, as the very cloak and costume of imperial enterprise. The main effort of architecture was to give an effect of dignity and permanence to the façades of the principal thoroughfares: the public buildings must dominate the compositions, numerous boulevards and avenues must concentrate the traffic at certain points and guide the stranger to the markets and amusements: where possible, as in the Chicago plan, by Messrs. Burnham and Bennett, avenues must be cut through the gridiron pattern of blocks in order to achieve these effects. If this imperial street system is somewhat arbitrary, and if the necessary work of grading, filling, demolishing, and purchasing existing property rights is extremely costly, the end, nevertheless, justifies the means﻿—the architecture impresses and awes a populace that shares vicariously in it glories. Should the effect prove a little too austere and formidable, the monuments will be offset with circuses and hippodromes.

In all this, the World’s Fair was a precise and classic example, for it reproduced in miniature the imperial order. When the panic of 1893 kept people away from the exhibitions of art, industry, and culture, sideshows were promptly introduced by the astute organizers. Beyond the serene classic façades, which recalled the elevation of a Marcus Aurelius, sprawled the barkers, the freaks, and the tricksters, whose gaudy booths might have reminded the spectator of the other side of the imperial shield﻿—the *gaminism* of Petronius Arbiter. The transformation of these white façades into the Gay White Ways came during the next decade; whilst the sideshows achieved a separate existence as “Coney Island.” On top of this came the development of the mildly gladiatorial spectacles of football and baseball: at first invented for playful exercise, they became a standard means of exhibition by more or less professional performers. The erection of numerous amphitheaters and arenas, such as the Yale Bowl, the Harvard Stadium, the Lewisohn Stadium, and their counterparts in the West, rounded out the imperial spectacle.

By a happy congruence of forces, the large-scale manufacture of Portland cement, and the reintroduction of the Roman method of concrete construction, came during the same period. Can anyone contemplate this scene and still fancy that imperialism was nothing more than a move for foreign markets and territories of exploitation? On the contrary, it was a tendency that expressed itself in every department of Western civilization, and if it appears most naked, perhaps, in America, that is only because, as in the earlier periods, there was so little here to stand in its way. Mr. Louis Sullivan might well complain, in *The Autobiography of an Idea*, that imperialism stifled the more creative modes of architecture which might have derived from our fine achievements in science, from our tentative experiments in democracy. It seems inevitable, however, that the dominant fact in our civilization should stamp the most important monuments and buildings with its image. In justice to the great professors of the classic style, Messrs. McKim and Burnham and Carrere and Hastings, one must admit that the age shaped them and chose them and used them for its ends. Their mode of building was almost unescapably determined by the milieu in which they worked.

===Chinese===
1890 至 1910 年间的二十年，见证了罗马样式作为帝国企业的正装与行头得到全面的恢复。建筑的主要努力，是给主要干道的立面赋予一种庄严而恒久的效果：公共建筑必须统摄整体构图，无数林荫大道与大街必须把交通汇聚到若干节点，引导异乡人走向市场与娱乐场所：凡有可能，如伯纳姆与本内特（Bennett）二位先生的芝加哥规划所示，便必须让大街穿透街块的网格格局，以收其效。如果说这套帝国式街道体系多少有些专断，如果说平整场地、填筑、拆除、收购既定产权等必要工程耗资极其浩大，目的终究证明手段正当——这建筑慑服并震慑着一班民众，而他们只能隔岸分享它的荣光。万一效果显得过于冷峻威严，还有竞技场和赛马场来为那些纪念碑作调剂。

在这一切之中，世博会是一个精确的古典范例，因为它把帝国秩序缩微地复现了出来。1893 年的恐慌使人们不来观看艺术、工业与文化的展览时，精明的组织者立刻引入了杂耍陪衬节目。在那些令人想起马可·奥勒留（Marcus Aurelius）式崇高风范的静穆古典立面之外，叫卖的、畸形秀的和耍把戏的漫地铺陈，他们花哨的棚屋或许会让观者想起帝国之盾的另一面——佩特罗尼乌斯·阿尔比特（Petronius Arbiter）的*顽童气*。这些白色立面蜕变为一条条「欢乐白光道」（Gay White Ways），是下一个十年的事；而那些陪衬节目则另行独立存在，成了「科尼岛」（Coney Island）。在此之上又发展出足球与棒球这类温和的角斗表演：起初是为游戏性运动而发明，后来却成了多少带有职业性质的表演者的标准展演手段。大量圆形剧场与竞技场的兴建——如耶鲁碗（Yale Bowl）、哈佛体育场（Harvard Stadium）、刘易森体育场（Lewisohn Stadium）及其在西部的对应物——使帝国的排场圆满收束。

由于种种力量的幸运会合，波特兰水泥的大规模制造与罗马混凝土工法的重新引入，恰好都发生在这一时期。目睹此情此景，谁还能把帝国主义仅仅想象成一场争夺海外市场与剥削疆域的举动？恰恰相反，它是一种在西方文明每个部门都有所表现的倾向；如果说它在美国显得最为赤裸，那只是因为，同较早的时期一样，这里几乎没有什么东西挡它的路。路易斯·沙利文（Louis Sullivan）先生在《一个观念的自传》（*The Autobiography of an Idea*）中抱怨说，帝国主义窒息了建筑中那些更具创造性的模式——它们本可以从我们在科学上的出色成就中、从我们在民主方面的初步实验中生长出来，这自是情理中事。然而，占支配地位的文明事实，势必要把它自己的形象烙在最重要的纪念碑与建筑上，这似乎无可逃避。持平而论，对麦金、伯纳姆、卡雷尔（Carrere）与哈斯廷斯（Hastings）这些古典风格的大宗师，必须承认：是时代塑造了他们，选择了他们，并为达到自己的目的而使用了他们。他们的营造模式，几乎不可避免地为他们工作于其中的环境所决定。
===Original===
The change in the social scene which favored an imperial setting was not without its effects upon the industries that supplied the materials for architecture, and upon the processes of building itself. Financial concentration in the stone quarries, for example, was abetted by the creation of a national system of rail transportation, and partly, perhaps, by the elaboration of the mechanical equipment for cutting and trimming stone beyond a point where a small plant could work economically. The result was that during this period numerous small local quarries, which had been called into existence by Richardson’s fine eye for color contrasts, were allowed to lapse. Vermont marble and Indiana limestone served better the traditions that had been created in the White City.

The carrying of coals to Newcastle is always a pathetic practice; it remained for the imperial age to make it a subject for boasting. Just as many Connecticut towns whose nearby fields are full of excellent granite boulders, boast a bank or a library of remote marble, so New York City, which has a solid foundation of schist, gneiss, and limestone, can point to only a handful of buildings, notably the College of the City of New York and Mr. Goodhue’s Church of the Intercession, in which these excellent local materials were used. The curious result of being able by means of railway transportation to draw upon the ends of the earth for materials has been, not variety, but monotony. Under the imperial order the architect was forced to design structures that were identical in style, treatment, and material, though they were placed thousands of miles apart and differed in every important function. This ignorance of regional resources is not incompatible with grand effects, or even on occasion with decently good architecture. But it does not profit by that fine adaptation to site, that justness of proportion in the size of window and slope of roof, which is an earnest of the architect’s mastery of the local situation. Substitute Manila for the military colony of Timgad, or Los Angeles for Alexandria, and it is plain that we have here another aspect of Ferrero’s generalization. Even architects whose place of work was nearer to the site of their buildings were, nevertheless, compelled to copy the style of the more successful practitioners in New York and Chicago.

In government, in industry, in architecture, the imperial age was one. The underlying policy of imperialism is to exploit the life and resources of separate regions for the benefit of the holders of privilege in the capital city. Under this rule, all roads lead literally to Rome. While, as the German historian, W. H. Riehl, points out, the provincial highroads served to bring the city out into the countryside, the railroads served to bring the major cities together and to drain the products of rural regions into the metropolis. It was no accident that the great triumphs of American architecture during the imperial period were the railroad stations; particularly the Pennsylvania and the Grand Central in New York, and the Union Station in Washington. Nor is it by mere chance that the Washington and the Pennsylvania stations are the monuments to two architects, McKim and Burnham, who worshiped most wholeheartedly at the imperial shrine. With capital insight, these men established the American Academy at Rome: they recognized their home.

===Chinese===
有利于帝国布景的社会场景变迁，也对供应建筑材料的各行业、对营造过程本身产生了影响。例如，采石业的金融集中，受到全国铁路运输体系创立的助长，或许也部分地因为切割修整石料的机械设备日趋精良，超出了小厂能够经济作业的限度。结果是，这一时期里，许多因理查森对色彩对比的慧眼而问世的当地小采石场，任其荒废了。佛蒙特的大理石和印第安纳的石灰岩，更能效忠白色城市所开创的传统。

把煤运往纽卡斯尔从来是一种可悲的营生；帝国时代却把它变成了夸耀的题目。正如康涅狄格的许多城镇，附近田野里满是优质的花岗岩巨砾，却夸耀一座远方大理石砌成的银行或图书馆；纽约城坐拥片岩、片麻岩和石灰岩的坚实地基，却只能数出寥寥几座用上这些优秀本地材料的建筑——著名者如纽约城市学院和古德休（Goodhue）先生的代祷教堂（Church of the Intercession）。凭借铁路运输得以从天涯海角汲取材料，其奇特的结果不是多样，而是单调。在帝国秩序之下，建筑师被迫设计出风格、处理与材料完全雷同的结构，尽管它们相距数千英里，一切重要功能皆不相同。这种对地区资源的无视，并非与宏大效果不相容，有时甚至不妨碍相当不错的建筑。但它得不到那种对场地的精妙适应，得不到窗幅与屋顶坡度之间那种恰到好处的比例——那正是建筑师娴熟驾驭当地情形的保证。试以马尼拉替换罗马的军垦殖民地廷加德（Timgad），以洛杉矶替换亚历山大港（Alexandria），便可明白，我们在这里又见到了费雷罗那句概括的另一面。就连工作地点离营造现场较近的建筑师，也不得不照搬纽约和芝加哥那些较为成功的同行们的风格。

在政府里，在产业里，在建筑里，帝国时代浑然一体。帝国主义的根本政策，是为都城中特权占有者的利益而榨取各地区的生命与资源。在这条法则之下，条条大道都实实在在地通向罗马。而且，正如德国历史学家 W. H. 里尔（W. H. Riehl）所指出的，昔日省际大道服务于把城市带向乡野，铁路却服务于把大城市连成一体，把乡村地区的物产抽进大都会。帝国时期美国建筑的大胜利恰是火车站：尤其是纽约的宾夕法尼亚车站和大中央车站，以及华盛顿的联合车站。华盛顿车站与宾夕法尼亚车站分别是麦金与伯纳姆两人的纪念碑，而这两位正是在帝国神殿前拜得最虔诚的人，这也绝非偶然。这班人以资本家的眼光在罗马创立了美国学院：他们认得自己的老家。

===Original===
Esthetically considered, it is true, perhaps, that the finest element in the Pennsylvania station is the train hall, where the architect has dealt sincerely with his steel elements and has not permitted himself to cast a fond, retrospective eye upon the Roman baths. When all allowances are made, however, there remains less for criticism in the railway stations and the stadiums﻿—those genuinely Roman bequests﻿—than in any of the other imperial monuments. Indeed, so well does Roman architecture lend itself to the railroad station that one of the prime virtues of such a building, namely ease of circulation, was even communicated to the New York Public Library, where it is nothing but a nuisance, since it both increases the amount of noise and diminishes the amount of space for reading rooms that are already overcrowded.

Here, indeed, is the capital defect of an established and formalized mode: it tends to make the architect think of a new problem in terms of an old solution for a different problem. Mr. Charles McKim, for example, indignantly withdrew from the competition over the New York Public Library because the demands of the librarian for a convenient and expeditious administration of his business interfered with the full-blown conception which Mr. McKim had in mind. All this happened after years of demonstration in the Boston Library of Messrs. McKim and White’s failure to meet that problem squarely; and it apparently was not affected by Mr. McKim’s experience with the great Columbia Library, which has ample space for everything except books. In short, the classic style served well enough only when the building to be erected had some direct relation to the needs and interests of the Roman world﻿—the concourse of idlers in the baths or the tiers of spectators in the circuses and hippodromes. When it came face to face with our own day, it had but little to say, and it said that badly, as anyone who will patiently examine the superimposed orders on the American Telegraph Building in New York will discover for himself.

===Chinese===
从美学上看，宾夕法尼亚车站最美的元素或许确实是列车大厅：在那里，建筑师真诚地对待他的钢构件，没有容许自己向罗马浴场投去一往情深的怀古一瞥。然而，纵使千般体谅，火车站与体育场——那些真正罗马式的遗产——之中可批评之处，仍少于其他任何帝国纪念碑。的确，罗马建筑之宜于火车站，竟到了这般地步：这类建筑的一大美德，即通行的便利，甚至传染给了纽约公共图书馆，而在那里它纯属祸害，因为它既加大了噪声，又缩减了本已过分拥挤的阅览室的空间。

这里正是一种既定化、形式化模式的头号缺陷：它往往使建筑师用一个针对另一问题的旧解法，来思考一个新问题。例如，查尔斯·麦金（Charles McKim）先生曾愤然退出纽约公共图书馆的竞赛，因为图书馆长提出的业务管理务求便捷高效的要求，妨碍了麦金先生心中那套盛开圆满的构想。这一切发生在麦金与怀特二位先生的波士顿图书馆多年示范其无力正视这一问题之后；而且显然也未受麦金先生经手哥伦比亚大图书馆一事的影响——那座图书馆样样都有充裕的空间，唯独书没有。简言之，只有当待建建筑与罗马世界的需要和利益有某种直接关系时——浴场里闲人的集会，竞技场与赛马场看台上的层层观众——古典风格才算差强人意。一旦与我们的时代面对面，它便几乎无话可说，仅有的几句还说得糟糕；谁肯耐心检视纽约美国电报大楼上层层叠加的柱式，谁就会自己发现这一点。
### III / 三



===Original===
With the transition from republican to imperial Rome, numerous monuments were erected to the Divine Caesar. Within a much shorter time than marked the growth of the imperial tradition in America, a similar edification of patriotic memories took place.

In the restoration of the original plan of Washington, which began in 1901, the axis of the plan was so altered as to make it pass through the Washington Monument; and at the same time the place of the Lincoln Memorial, designed by the late Mr. Henry Bacon, a pupil of Mr. McKim’s, was assigned. This was the first of a whole series of temples devoted to the national deities. In the Lincoln Memorial, in the McKinley Memorial at Niles, Ohio, in the Hall of Fame at New York University, and in their prototype, Grant’s Tomb, one feels not the living beauty of our American past, but the mortuary air of archaeology. The America that Lincoln was bred in, the homespun and humane and humorous America that he wished to preserve, has nothing in common with the sedulously classic monument that was erected to his memory. Who lives in that shrine, I wonder﻿—Lincoln, or the men who conceived it: the leader who beheld the mournful victory of the Civil War, or the generation that took pleasure in the mean triumph of the Spanish-American exploit, and placed the imperial standard in the Philippines and the Caribbean?

On the plane of private citizenship, a similar movement took place: while before 1890 one can count the tombs in our cemeteries that boast loudly of the owner’s earthly possessions and power, from that time onward the miniature temple-mausoleum becomes more and more frequent. In fact, an entire history of architecture could be deduced from our cemeteries; all that has so far been described could be marked in the progress from the simple slab, carved in almost Attic purity with a weeping willow or a cubistic cherub, that characterized the eighteenth century, to the bad lettering and the more awkward headstones of the early nineteenth century; and from this to the introduction of polished granite and iron ornament in the post-Civil War cemetery, down to the mechanically perfect mausoleum, where the corpses are packed like the occupants of a subway train, that some of our more effusively progressive communities boast of today. As we live, so we die: no wonder Shelley described Hell as a place much like London.

===Chinese===
随着罗马由共和转入帝制，为神圣的恺撒竖起了无数纪念碑。美国帝国传统的成长费时更长，而在比之短得多的时间内，一场类似的爱国记忆圣化便告完成。

1901 年开始的华盛顿原初规划的复兴中，规划的中轴线被改动，使之穿过华盛顿纪念碑；与此同时，已故的亨利·培根（Henry Bacon）先生——麦金先生的一位门徒——所设计的林肯纪念堂的位置也告划定。这是一整系列奉祀国家神祇的庙宇中的第一座。在林肯纪念堂，在俄亥俄州奈尔斯（Niles）的麦金莱纪念堂，在纽约大学的名人堂，以及在它们的原型格兰特墓中，人们感到的不是我们美国过去活的美好，而是考古学的墓园气息。林肯生于其间的那个美国，那个家织土布、人道而幽默的美国，那个他想保存下来的美国，与那座为纪念他而竖起的刻意求工的古典纪念碑，毫无共通之处。我不禁纳闷，住在那座圣祠里的是谁——是林肯，还是构想它的那班人：是目睹内战惨胜的那位领袖，还是在美西战争的卑劣胜利中取乐、把帝国军旗插上菲律宾与加勒比海的那一代人？

在私人公民的层面上，一场类似的运动也在进行：1890 年以前，我们墓园中高声夸耀墓主世间财货与权势的陵墓尚屈指可数，从那时起，小型神庙式陵堂便日益常见。事实上，一部完整的建筑史可以从我们的墓园里推演出来；迄今为止所描述的一切，都可以标记在这一进程之中：从十八世纪那种以近乎阿提卡式的纯净雕着一株垂柳或一个立体派小天使的简单石板，到十九世纪早期拙劣的刻字和更见笨拙的墓碑；再从那里到内战后墓园中抛光花岗岩与铁饰的引入，直至机械完美的陵堂——尸体像地铁车厢里的乘客一样打包堆放——我们某些更为热情进取的社区今天正引以为豪。我们怎样生活，便怎样死去：难怪雪莱把地狱描写成一个酷似伦敦的地方。

===Original===
The Roman development of New York, Chicago, Washington, and the lesser metropolises, had an important effect upon the homes of the people. Historically, the imperial monument and the slum-tenement go hand in hand. The same process that creates an unearned increment for the landlords who possess favored sites, contributes a generous quota﻿—which might be called the unearned excrement﻿—of depression, overcrowding, and bad living, in the dormitory districts of the city. This had happened in imperial Rome; it had happened again in Paris under Napoleon III, where Haussmann’s sweeping reconstructions created new slums in the districts behind the grand avenues, quite as bad, if far less obvious, as those that had been cleared away; and it happened once again in our American cities. Whereas in Rome a certain limit, however, was placed upon the expansion of the city because of the low development of vehicular traffic, the rise of mechanical transportation placed no bounds at all on the American city. If Rome was forced to create huge engineering projects like aqueducts and sewers in order to cleanse the inhabitants and remove the offal of its congested districts, the American city followed the example of the modern Romes like London and Paris by devising man-sewers, in which the mass of plebeians could be daily drained back and forth between their dormitories and their factories.

So far from relieving congestion, these colossal pieces of engineering only made more of it possible: by pouring more feeder lines into the central district of New York, Boston, Chicago, or where you will, rapid transit increased the housing congestion at one end and the business-congestion at the other. As for the primary sewer system devised for the imperial metropolis, it could scarcely even claim, with rapid transit, that it was a valuable commercial investment. The water outlets of New York are so thoroughly polluted that not merely have the shad and the oyster beds vanished from the Hudson River, where both once flourished, but it is a serious question whether the tides can continue to transport their vast load of sewage without a preliminary reduction of its content. Like the extension of the water conduits into the Adirondacks, all these necessary little improvements add to the per capita cost of living in an imperial metropolis, without providing a single benefit that a smaller city with no need for such improvements does not enjoy. In the matter of public parks, for example, the Committee on Congestion in New York, in 1911, calculated that the park space needed for the East Side alone, on the scale provided by the city of Hartford, would be greater than the entire area of Manhattan Island. In short, even for its bare utilitarian requirements, the mass-city, as the Germans call it, costs more and gives less than communities which have not had imperial greatness inflicted upon them.

===Chinese===
纽约、芝加哥、华盛顿及一众次级都会的罗马式发展，对民众的住宅产生了重要影响。从历史上说，帝国纪念碑与贫民窟公寓携手并行。同一个过程，一面为坐拥黄金地段的地主创造不劳而获的增值，一面在城市宿舍区里慷慨地贡献出一大份额——不妨称之为不劳而获的排泄物——的消沉、拥挤与恶劣生活。这在帝国罗马发生过；在拿破仑三世治下的巴黎又发生过，奥斯曼大刀阔斧的重建，在宏伟大街背后的街区里造出了新的贫民窟，同清走的旧贫民窟一样恶劣，只是远不那么显眼；而在我们的美国城市里，它再一次发生了。不过，在罗马，由于车辆交通欠发达，城市的扩张尚有某种限度；机械交通的兴起，却给美国城市不设任何边界。如果说罗马被迫建造引水道和下水道之类的巨型工程来洁净居民、清运拥挤城区的秽物，美国城市则效法伦敦、巴黎这些现代罗马，设计了「人力下水道」，让大批平民每天在其宿舍与工厂之间来回抽排。

这些庞然大物般的工程远没有缓解拥挤，反而使更大的拥挤成为可能：往纽约、波士顿、芝加哥或随便哪个城市的中心区灌入更多的辐合线，快速交通便在一端加重了住宅拥挤，在另一端加重了商务拥挤。至于为帝国大都会设计的主下水道系统，它连同快速交通一起，几乎连「有价值的商业投资」都称不上。纽约的出水口污染得如此彻底，以至于不仅西鲱和牡蛎床已从哈得孙河消失——两者都曾在那里繁盛——而且潮水能否继续运送它那庞大的污水量而不预先减少其含量，也已成了严肃的问题。像输水管道延伸进阿迪朗达克山区一样，所有这些必要的小小改良，都增加了帝国大都会的人均生活成本，却没有提供任何一种好处，是无需这些改良的较小城市所不享有的。例如在公共公园方面，纽约拥挤问题委员会 1911 年测算出，仅东区一地按哈特福德市提供的标准所需的公园面积，便超过整个曼哈顿岛的面积。简言之，即便只就其赤裸裸的实用需求而言，这座被德国人唤作「大众城市」的巨城，也比那些未被强加帝国伟业的社区花费更多、给予更少。
===Original===
As to the more positive improvements under the imperial regime, history leaves no doubt as to their dubious character, and current observation only reinforces history’s lesson. In discussing the growth of the tenement in Rome after the Great Fire, Friedlander says:

> “The motives for piling up storeys were as strong as ever: the site for Caesar’s Forum had cost over £875,000 compensation to tenants and ground landlords. Rome had loftier houses than modern capital. A disproportionately large part of the area available for building was monopolized by the few, in consequence of the waste of space in the plethoric architecture of the day, and a very considerable portion was swallowed up by the public places, such as the imperial forums, which took up six hectares, as well as by the traffic regulations and extensions of the streets. The transformation and decoration of Rome by the Caesars enhanced the scarcity of housing, as did Napoleon III’s improvements in Paris. A further adjutory cause of the increase in the price of dwellings was the habit of speculation in house property (which Crassus had practiced in great style) and the monopoly of the proprietors, in consequence of which houses were let and sublet.”

===Chinese===
至于帝国体制下那些较为积极的改良，历史对其可疑的性质不留怀疑的余地，当下的观察只是进一步印证了历史的教训。谈到大火之后罗马多屋住宅的增长时，弗里德兰德（Friedlander）说道：

> 「加高楼层的动机一如既往地强烈：恺撒广场的地基，曾向租户和地主支付了超过 875,000 英镑的补偿金。罗马拥有比现代首都更高耸的房屋。可供建造的地面中，不成比例的一大部分为少数人所垄断，这是当日臃肿建筑浪费空间的结果；还有相当大的一部分被公共场所吞噬，例如占了六公顷的帝国广场群，又如交通管制和街道的拓宽。诸恺撒对罗马的改造与装饰加剧了住房的匮乏，正如拿破仑三世在巴黎的改良一样。住宅价格上涨的又一个助力，是房产投机的积习（克拉苏就曾大张旗鼓地实践过），以及业主的垄断，其结果是房屋被层层转租。」

===Original===
It would be tedious to draw out the parallel: given similar social conditions in America we have not been able to escape the same social results, even down to the fact that the palliatives of private philanthropy flourish here again as they had not flourished anywhere on the same scale since the Roman Empire. So much for imperial greatness. When an architect like Mr. Edward Bennett can say, as he did in *The Significance of the Fine Arts*: “House the people densely, if necessary, but conserve great areas for recreation,” we need not be in doubt as to who will profit by the density and who will profit, at the other end, by the recreation. It is not merely that the park must be produced to remedy the congestion: it is even more that the congestion must be produced in order to provide for the park. To profit by both the disease and the remedy is one of the masterstrokes of imperialist enterprise. Mr. Daniel Burnham said of the World’s Fair, according to Mr. Bennett and Mr. Charles Moore, “that it is what the Romans would have wished to create in permanent form.” One may say of our imperial cities that they are what the Romans did create﻿—but whether the form will be permanent or not is a matter we may leave to the sardonic attentions of history.

For my own part, I think we have at last acquired a criterion which will enable us to sum up the architecture of the imperial age, and deal justly with these railroad stations and stadiums, these sewers and circuses, these aqueducts and parkways and grand avenues. Our imperial architecture is an architecture of compensation: it provides grandiloquent stones for people who have been deprived of bread and sunlight and all that keeps man from becoming vile. Behind the monumental façades of our metropolises trudges a landless proletariat, doomed to the servile routine of the factory system; and beyond the great cities lies a countryside whose goods are drained away, whose children are uprooted from the soil on the prospect of easy gain and endless amusements, and whose remaining cultivators are steadily drifting into the ranks of an abject tenantry. This is not a casual observation: it is the translation of the last three census reports into plain English. Can one take the pretensions of this architecture seriously; can one worry about its esthetics or take full delight in such finer forms as Mr. Pope’s Temple of the Scottish Rite in Washington, or Mr. Bacon’s Lincoln Memorial? Yes, perhaps﻿—if one refuses to look beyond the mask.

===Chinese===
把这组对应一一铺陈出来未免乏味：在相似的社会条件之下，美国未能逃脱相同的社会结果，甚至直到这样一个细节：私人慈善的止痛剂在此再度兴旺，其规模之大，自罗马帝国以来无处可及。帝国的伟大，如此而已。当爱德华·贝内特（Edward Bennett）先生这样的建筑师说出——如他在《美术的意义》（*The Significance of the Fine Arts*）中所说——「必要时尽管让人民住得稠密，但要保存大片区域用于娱乐」时，谁将从稠密中获利，谁又将在另一头从娱乐中获利，我们无须存疑。不仅仅是因为必须造出公园来医治拥挤：更是因为必须制造出拥挤，以便供给公园。从疾病与药方两头同时渔利，是帝国主义企业的大手笔之一。据贝内特先生和查尔斯·摩尔（Charles Moore）先生记载，丹尼尔·伯纳姆先生谈及世博会时曾说：「这正是罗马人本来希望以永久形式创造出来的东西。」关于我们的帝国城市，倒可以说，它们正是罗马人确实创造出来的东西——至于这形式是否永久，我们不妨留给历史那冷嘲式的垂注。

就我而言，我想我们终于得到了一个标准，可以总评帝国时代的建筑，公正地对待这些火车站与体育场、这些下水道与竞技场、这些引水道、林园大道和宏伟大街。我们的帝国建筑是一种补偿的建筑：它为那些被剥夺了面包、阳光以及一切使人免于卑污之物的人们，提供夸夸其谈的石头。在我们大都会的纪念立面背后，跋涉着一个无地的无产阶级，命定受着工厂制度的奴役苦役；在大城市之外，躺着一片物产被抽干的乡村，其儿女被轻易发财和无尽娱乐的前景连根拔离泥土，其残存的耕作者则不断坠入卑贱佃农的行列。这不是一句随口的观察：这是把最近三份人口普查报告译成了平实的英语。谁能拿这种建筑的僭妄当真？谁能操心它的美学，或尽情欣赏诸如波普（Pope）先生在华盛顿的苏格兰礼仪圣殿（Temple of the Scottish Rite）、培根先生的林肯纪念堂这类更精微的形式？也许能——只要此人拒绝看破面具之外。

===Original===
Even in some of its proudest buildings, the imperial show wears thin; and one need not peer into the slums beyond in order to realize its defects. The rear of the Metropolitan Museum or the Brooklyn Museum, for example, might be the rear of a row of Bronx tenements or Long Island City factories, so gaunt and barren and hideous is their aspect. If the imperial age was foreshadowed in the World’s Fair, it has received its apotheosis in the museum. In contrast to the local museums one still finds occasionally in Europe, which are little more than extensions of the local curio cabinet, the imperial museum is essentially a loot-heap, a comprehensive repository for plunder. The sage Viollet-le-Duc once patly said that he preferred to see his apples hanging on a tree, rather than arranged in rows in the fruit shop: but the animus of the museum is to value the plucked fruit more than the tree that bore it.

Into the museum come the *disjecta membra* of other lands, other cultures, other civilizations. All that had once been a living faith and practice is here reduced to a separate specimen, pattern, or form. For the museum, the world of art has already been created: the future is restricted to a duplication of the perfected past. This animus is identic with that which made the Romans so skillful in copying Greek statues and so dull in carving their own; a desirable habit of humility were it not for the fact that the works of art in the past could not have been created had our ancestors been so punctual in respect to finished designs. The one thing the museum cannot attempt to do is to supply a soil for living art: all that it can present is a pattern for reproduction. To the extent that an insincere or imitative art is better than no art at all, the Imperial Age marked an advance: to the extent, however, that a living art is a fresh gesture of the spirit, the museum confessed all too plainly that the age had no fresh gestures to make; on that score, it was a failure, and the copying of period furniture and the design of period architecture were the livid proofs of that failure.

The museum is a manifestation of our curiosity, our acquisitiveness, our essentially predatory culture; and these qualities were copiously exhibited in the architecture of imperialism. It would be foolish to reproach the great run of architects for exploiting the characteristics of their age; for even those who in belief and design have remained outside the age﻿—such resolute advocates of a medieval polity as Dr. Ralph Adams Cram﻿—have not been able to divert its currents. In so far as we have learned to care more for empire than for a community of freemen, living the good life, more for dominion over palm and pine than for the humane discipline of ourselves, the architect has but enshrined our desires. The opulence, the waste of resources and energies, the perversion of human effort represented in this architecture are but the outcome of our general scheme of working and living. Architecture, like government, is about as good as a community deserves. The shell that we create for ourselves marks our spiritual development as plainly as that of a snail denotes its species. If sometimes architecture becomes frozen music, we have ourselves to thank when it is a pompous blare of meaningless sounds.

===Chinese===
甚至在它某些最引以为豪的建筑里，帝国的排场也显出了磨损；人们无须窥探远处的贫民窟，便能意识到它的缺陷。譬如，大都会博物馆或布鲁克林博物馆的背面，说不定就是布朗克斯一排公寓或长岛市一排工厂的背面——它们的面目就是这般嶙峋、荒芜而丑陋。如果说帝国时代在世博会中得到了预示，它在博物馆里则升入了神格。与欧洲至今偶尔仍可见到的地方博物馆——它们不过是地方珍玩柜的延伸——相对照，帝国博物馆本质上是一座战利品堆，一个包罗万象的赃物仓库。智者维奥莱-勒-杜克曾有妙语，说他宁愿看着自己的苹果挂在树上，也不愿看它们在水果店里排成一行：而博物馆的心机，却是重摘下的果实，轻结果实的树。

进入博物馆的，是其他土地、其他文化、其他文明的零落残肢（*disjecta membra*）。一切曾是活的信仰与实践的东西，在这里被还原成一个个孤立的标本、图样或形式。对博物馆来说，艺术的世界已经被创造完毕：未来仅限于对已臻完美之过去的复制。这种心机，与使罗马人如此娴于复制希腊雕像、又如此钝于雕刻自己雕像的心机一模一样；这本是一种可取的谦卑习惯，倘若不是有这样一个事实：倘若我们的祖先对既成的图样这般亦步亦趋，往昔的艺术品根本无从创造。博物馆唯一无力一试的事，是为活的艺术提供一方土壤：它能呈献的，只有一个供复制的图样。就虚伪的或模仿的艺术胜于全然没有艺术而言，帝国时代标志了一种进步；然而，就活的艺术乃是精神的一次崭新姿态而言，博物馆再直白不过地招认：这个时代没有崭新的姿态可作；在这一项上，它是一个失败，而各时代式样家具的仿制与各时代式样建筑的设计，便是这一失败惨白的明证。

博物馆是我们的好奇心、我们的占有欲、我们那本质上掠夺成性的文化的一个显现；这些品质在帝国主义的建筑中得到了酣畅的展览。因利用时代特征而责备大多数建筑师，是愚蠢的；因为就连那些在信念与设计上都置身时代之外的人——例如拉尔夫·亚当斯·克拉姆（Ralph Adams Cram）博士这样坚决的中世纪政体鼓吹者——也未能使它的潮流转向。既然我们学会了对帝国的关怀胜过对一个过着美好生活的自由人共同体的关怀，对棕榈与松柏之统治的看重胜过对自身人性自律的看重，建筑师不过是把我们的欲望供上了神龛。这种建筑所代表的豪奢、资源与精力的浪费、人力的扭曲，不过是我们总体工作与生活格局的结局。建筑同政府一样，其好坏全看一个社区配得上什么。我们为自己营造的外壳，标示着我们的精神发展，正如蜗牛的壳标示它的种属。建筑若有时竟成了凝固的音乐，那么当它化作一场浮夸而空洞的声响轰鸣时，我们只能感谢自己。

## I The Medieval Tradition / 中世纪传统

### I / 一

===Original===
For a hundred years or so after its settlement, there lived and flourished in America a type of community which was rapidly disappearing in Europe. This community was embodied in villages and towns whose mummified remains even today have a rooted dignity that the most gigantic metropolises do not often possess. If we would understand the architecture of America in a period when good building was almost universal, we must understand something of the kind of life that this community fostered.

The capital example of the medieval tradition lies in the New England village.

There are two or three things that stand in the way of our seeing the life of a New England village; and one of them is the myth of the pioneer, the conception of the first settlers as a free band of “Americans” throwing off the bedraggled garments of Europe and starting life afresh in the wilderness. So far from giving birth to a new life, the settlement of the northern American seaboard prolonged for a little while the social habits and economic institutions which were fast crumbling away in Europe, particularly in England. In the villages of the New World there flickered up the last dying embers of the medieval order.

Whereas in England the common lands were being confiscated for the benefit of an aristocracy, and the arable turned into sheep-runs for the profit of the great proprietors, in New England the common lands were reestablished with the founding of a new settlement. In England the depauperate peasants and yeomen were driven into the large towns to become the casual workers, menials, and soldiers; in New England, on the other hand, it was at first only with threats of punishment and conscription that the town workers were kept from going out into the countryside to seek a more independent living from the soil. Just as the archaic speech of the Elizabethans has lingered in the Kentucky Mountains, so the Middle Ages at their best lingered along the coast of Appalachia; and in the organization of our New England villages one sees a greater resemblance to the medieval Utopia of Sir Thomas More than to the classic republic in the style of Montesquieu, which was actually founded in the eighteenth century.

===Chinese===
在殖民定居之后的百来年间，一种正在欧洲迅速消失的共同体类型，在美国存续并兴盛。这种共同体体现在一座座村镇之中；即便今天，这些村镇木乃伊般的遗骸仍带着一种根深蒂固的尊严，那是最大的几个大都会也不常具备的。我们要想理解美国建筑在那良好营造几乎蔚然成风的年代，就必须对这种共同体所滋养的生活方式有所了解。

中世纪传统最典型的例子，就在新英格兰的村庄里。

有两三样东西阻碍我们看清新英格兰村庄的生活；其中之一便是拓荒者的神话——把首批定居者设想成一伙甩掉欧洲褴褛衣衫、在荒野里重起炉灶的自由「美国人」。北美海岸的殖民非但没有催生一种新生活，反而把正在欧洲、尤其在英格兰迅速崩解的社会习惯与经济制度又延续了一小段时日。在新大陆的村庄里，中世纪秩序最后的余烬闪了闪微光。

在英格兰，公地正被没收以肥贵族，耕地正被变成大庄园主牟利的大羊圈；而在新英格兰，每逢建立新的殖民村镇，公地便被重新确立。在英格兰，赤贫的农民和自耕农被驱入大城镇，沦为临时工、仆役和士兵；在新英格兰，情形恰恰相反，起初要靠惩罚和征召的威胁，才能阻止城里的工人跑到乡下去，从土地里谋一份更独立的生活。正如伊丽莎白时代的古老语言在肯塔基山区留存下来一样，中世纪在其鼎盛时期的风貌也存留在阿巴拉契亚（Appalachia）海岸一带；在我们新英格兰村庄的组织里，人们看到的与其说是十八世纪实际建立的那种孟德斯鸠（Montesquieu）式的古典共和国，不如说更接近托马斯·莫尔爵士（Sir Thomas More）笔下的中世纪乌托邦。

===Original===
The colonists who sought to establish permanent communities﻿—as distinct from those who erected only trading posts﻿—were not a little like those whom the cities of Greece used to plant about the Mediterranean and the Black Sea littoral. Like the founders of the “Ancient City,” the Puritans first concerned themselves to erect an altar, or rather, to lay the foundations for an edifice which denied the religious value of altars. In the crudest of “smoaky wigwams,” an early observer notes, the Puritans remember to “sing psalms, pray, and praise their God”; and although we of today may regard their religion as harsh and naysaying, we cannot forget that it was a central point of their existence and not an afterthought piled as it were on material prosperity for the sake of a good appearance. Material goods formed the basis, but not the end, of their life.

The meetinghouse determined the character and limits of the community. As Weeden says in his excellent Economic and Social History of New England, the settlers “laid out the village in the best order to attain two objects: first, the tillage and culture of the soil; second, the maintenance of a ‘civil and religious society.’ ” Around the meetinghouse the rest of the community crystallized in a definite pattern, tight and homogeneous.

The early provincial village bears another resemblance to the early Greek city: it does not continue to grow at such a pace that it either becomes overcrowded within or spills beyond its limits into dejected suburbs; still less does it seek what we ironically call greatness by increasing the number of its inhabitants. When the corporation has a sufficient number of members, that is to say, when the land is fairly occupied, and when the addition of more land would unduly increase the hardship of working it from the town, or would spread out the farmers, and make it difficult for them to attend to their religious and civil duties, the original settlement throws out a new shoot. So Charlestown threw off Woburn; so Dedham colonized Medfield; so Lynn founded Nahant.

The Puritans knew and applied a principle that Plato had long ago pointed out in The Republic, namely, that an intelligent and socialized community will continue to grow only as long as it can remain a unit and keep up its common institutions. Beyond that point growth must cease, or the community will disintegrate and cease to be an organic thing. Economically, this method of community-development kept land values at a properly low level, and prevented the engrossing of land for the sake of a speculative rise. The advantage of the Puritan method of settlement comes out plainly when one contrasts it with the trader’s paradise of Manhattan; for by the middle of the seventeenth century all the land on Manhattan Island was privately owned, although only a small part of it was cultivated, and so eagerly had the teeth of monopoly bitten into this fine morsel that there was already a housing-shortage.

===Chinese===
那些寻求建立永久共同体的殖民者——有别于那些只立起商站的人——与希腊人昔日植民于地中海和黑海沿岸的城市颇有几分相像。像「古城」的奠基者一样，清教徒首先操心的是筑一座祭坛，或者更确切地说，为一座否定祭坛之宗教价值的建筑奠下基础。一位早期观察者写道，在最简陋的「烟熏火燎的小屋」里，清教徒也不忘「唱诗篇、祷告、赞美他们的上帝」；尽管今天的我们也许视他们的宗教严苛而多否定，我们不能忘记，它是他们生存的中枢，而不是为了体面而事后堆砌在物质繁荣之上的点缀。物质财富是他们生活的基础，却不是他们生活的目的。

聚会所决定了这个共同体的性质与界限。正如威登（Weeden）在他那部出色的《新英格兰经济与社会史》（Economic and Social History of New England）中所说，定居者们「以最好的秩序规划村镇，以求达到两个目标：第一，土地的耕作与培育；第二，维系一个『公民与宗教的社会』」。聚会所四周，共同体的其余部分按一种确定的图式结晶而成，紧凑而同质。

早期的外省村庄与早期的希腊城邦还有一层相像：它不以那样的速度持续增长，以致内部拥挤不堪，或溢出边界、漫成萎靡的郊区；它更不靠增加人口去追求我们冷嘲式地称为「伟大」的东西。当法人团体有了足够数目的成员，也就是说，当土地被相当充分地占用，当再添土地便会过度加重从镇上前往耕作的辛劳，或者会把农户摊薄、使他们难以兼顾宗教与公民义务时，原来的定居点便抽出一根新枝。查尔斯敦（Charlestown）就这样甩出了沃本（Woburn）；戴德姆（Dedham）就这样殖民了梅德菲尔德（Medfield）；林恩（Lynn）就这样建立了纳亨特（Nahant）。

清教徒懂得并应用了柏拉图（Plato）在《理想国》（The Republic）中早就指出的原则：一个明智而社会化的共同体，只有在还能保持一个统一体、还能维持其公共制度时，才继续生长。越过这一点，生长就必须停止，否则共同体便会解体，不再是一个有机体。在经济上，这种共同体发展的方法把地价保持在适度的低位，并防止了为投机上涨而圈占土地。把清教徒的殖民方法同曼哈顿这个商人的天堂对照一下，它的长处便一目了然；因为到十七世纪中叶，曼哈顿岛上的全部土地都已归了私人，尽管开垦的只有一小部分，而且垄断的牙齿咬进这块肥肉如此急切，以至于那时就已经出现了房荒。

===Original===
One more point of resemblance: all the inhabitants of an early New England village were copartners in a corporation; they admitted into the community only as many members as they could assimilate. This copartnership was based upon a common sense as to the purpose of the community, and upon a roughly equal division of the land into individual plots taken in freehold, and a share of the common fields, of which there might be half a dozen or more.

There are various local differences in the apportionment of the land. In many cases, the minister and deacons have a larger share than the rest of the community; but in Charlestown, for example, the poorest had six or seven acres of meadow and twenty-five or thereabouts of upland; and this would hold pretty well throughout the settlements. Not merely is membership in the community guarded: the right of occupying and transferring the land is also restricted, and again and again, in the face of the General Assembly, the little villages make provisions to keep the land from changing hands without the consent of the corporation; “it being our real intent,” as the burghers of Watertown put it, to “sitt down there close togither.”

These regulations have a positive side as well; for in some cases the towns helped the poorer members of the corporation to build houses, and as a new member was voted into the community, lots were assigned immediately, without further ado. A friend of mine has called this system “Yankee communism,” and I cheerfully bring the institution to the attention of those who do not realize upon what subversive principles Americanism, historically, rests.

What is true of the seventeenth century in New England holds good for the eighteenth century in the Moravian settlements of Pennsylvania; and it is doubtless true for many another obscure colony; for the same spirit lingered, with a parallel result in architecture and industry, in the utopian communities of the nineteenth century. It is pretty plain that this type of pioneering, this definite search for the good life, was conducted on an altogether different level from the ruthless exploitation of the individual muckers and scavengers who hit the trail west of the Alleghanies. Such renewals of the earlier European culture as the Bach Festival at Bethlehem give us a notion of the cultural values which the medieval community carried over from the Old World to the New. There is some of this spirit left even in the architecture of the Shaker community at Mount Lebanon, New York, which was built as late as the nineteenth century.

In contrast to the New England village-community was the trading post. Of this nature were the little towns in the New Netherlands which were planted there by the Dutch West India Company: the settlers were for the most part either harassed individuals who were lured to the New World by the prospects of a good living, or people of established rank who were tempted to leave the walks of commerce for the dignities and affluences that were attached to the feudal tenure of the large estates that lined the Hudson.

The germs of town life came over with these people, and sheer necessity turned part of their energies to agriculture, but they did not develop the close village-community we find in New England; and though New Amsterdam was a replica of the Old World port, with its gabled brick houses, and its well-banked canals and fine gardens, it left no decided pattern on the American scene. It is only the country architecture of the Dutch which survives as either a relic or a memory. These trading posts like Manhattan and Fort Orange were, as Messrs. Petersen and Edwards have shown in their study of New York as an Eighteenth Century Municipality, medieval in their economy: numerous guild and civic regulations which provided for honest weight and measure and workmanship continued in force within the town. In their external dealings, on the other hand, the practice of the traders was sharp, and every man was for himself. Beginning its life by bargaining in necessities, the trading post ends by making a necessity of bargaining; and it was the impetus from its original commercial habits which determined the characteristics of the abortive city plan that was laid down for Manhattan Island in 1811. Rich as the Dutch precedent is in individual farmhouses, it brings us no pattern, such as we find in New England, for the community as a whole.

===Chinese===
还有一层相像：早期新英格兰村庄的全部居民都是同一个法人团体里的合伙人；他们只吸收自己消化得了的成员进入共同体。这种合伙关系奠基于对共同体目的的共同判断，奠基于把土地大致均等地划分为一块块以自由保有权持有的宅地，外加公有田地上的一份份额——这样的公有田地可能有六块或者更多。

土地的分配存在种种地方差异。许多地方，牧师和执事分得比共同体里其他人多；但在查尔斯敦，最穷的人也有六七英亩草场和二十五英亩上下的高地；这一标准在各殖民村镇普遍通行。不但共同体的成员资格受到守护：占用和转让土地的权利也受到限制；一次又一次，这些小村庄顶着殖民地议会，作出规定，不让土地未经法人团体同意便易手；用沃特敦（Watertown）市民的话说，「我们真实的意图」是「紧紧挨着坐落在一起」。

这些规章还有积极的一面；因为有些镇子帮助法人团体里较穷的成员盖房，而新成员一经投票接纳进共同体，立刻便分得宅地，绝无拖延。我的一位朋友把这套制度称作「扬基共产主义」，我乐于一提这个机构，请那些不明白美国精神在历史上究竟立足于何等颠覆性原则的人注意。

新英格兰十七世纪的情形，同样适用于十八世纪宾夕法尼亚的摩拉维亚殖民地；无疑也适用于许多别的无名的殖民地；因为同样的精神一直存留到十九世纪的乌托邦共同体里，在建筑与工业上产生了平行的结果。相当明显，这种类型的拓殖、这种对美好生活的明确追寻，与那些在阿勒格尼山以西闯出小径的个体掘金者和拾荒者的无情榨取，全然不在同一个层面上。伯利恒（Bethlehem）的巴赫音乐节这类早期欧洲文化的复苏，让我们约略领会到中世纪共同体从旧大陆带到新大陆的文化价值。甚至迟至十九世纪才建造的纽约州黎巴嫩山（Mount Lebanon）震颤派社区的建筑里，也还留着几分这种精神。

与新英格兰的村庄共同体相对的，是商站。荷属新尼德兰那些由荷兰西印度公司植下的村镇便属此类：定居者大抵或是被好生计的前景诱到新大陆来的受尽困顿的个人，或是那些既有的身份等级的人——他们受诱离开商业行当，去谋哈德逊河两岸大庄园封建保有制所附带的体面与富足。

城市生活的胚芽随这些人一同渡海而来，而纯粹出于必需，他们把一部分精力转向了农业，但他们并没有发展出我们在新英格兰见到的那种紧密的村庄共同体；尽管新阿姆斯特丹是旧世界港埠的翻版，有山墙的砖屋、护岸齐整的运河和精美的花园，它却没有在美国景象上留下确定的图式。荷兰人的建筑只有乡村部分留存下来，或为遗物，或为记忆。正如彼得森（Petersen）与爱德华兹（Edwards）两位先生在他们对《作为十八世纪市政体的纽约》（New York as an Eighteenth Century Municipality）的研究中所表明的，曼哈顿和奥兰治堡（Fort Orange）这类商站在经济上是中世纪的：城镇里继续施行着大量关于度量衡与工艺之诚实的行会与市政规章。另一方面，在对外的交易上，商人们的行径是苛刻的，人人各自为战。以生活必需品的讨价还价开始其生涯的商站，最终把讨价还价变成了生活必需；而 1811 年为曼哈顿岛制定的那个流产的城市规划的种种特征，正是由它原先商业习惯的惯性决定的。荷兰先例在个体农舍上尽管丰富，它却没有给我们带来新英格兰那种关于整体共同体的图式。

### II / 二

===Original===
Since we are accustomed to look upon the village as a quaint primitive relic of a bygone age, we do not readily see that its form was dictated by social and economic conditions. Where the village had to defend itself against Indians, it was necessary to lay it out completely, so that it might be surrounded by a stockade, and so that the meetinghouse might be such a rallying center as the bell-tower or the castle was in Europe, or as the high temple site was in classic times. But in the eighteenth century the Indian figured less in the scheme of colonial life, and along the seacoast and river﻿—as at Wells Beach in Maine or Litchfield in Connecticut﻿—the village became a long strip upon a highroad, and the arable land stretched in narrow plots from the house to the water, so that the farmer might better protect his crops and his livestock from the fox, the wolf, the woodchuck, the hawk, the skunk, and the deer.

I emphasize these points of structure because of the silly notion superficial observers sometimes carry away from the villages of Europe or New England; namely, that their irregularity is altogether capricious and uneconomical, associated only with the vagaries of the straying cow. It would be more correct to say that the precise reverse was true. The inequality in size and shape of plots shows always that attention was paid to the function the land was to perform, rather than to the mere possession of property. Thus, there was a difference in size between home lots, which were always seated in the village, and purely agricultural tracts of land, which were usually on the outskirts; and in Dedham, for example, married men had home lots of twelve acres, while bachelors received only eight. Another reason for the compactness of the village was a decree of the General Court in Massachusetts, in 1635, that no dwelling should be placed more than half a mile from the meetinghouse in any new plantation. Even irregularities in the layout and placement of houses, which cannot be referred to such obvious points as these, very often derive from an attempt to break the path of the wind, to get a good exposure in summer, or to profit by a view.

All this was genuine community planning. It did not go by this name, perhaps, but it achieved the result.

===Chinese===
由于我们习惯于把村庄看作逝去年代的一种古雅而原始的遗物，我们不容易看出，它的形式是由社会与经济条件规定的。在村庄必须防御印第安人的地方，就必须把它完整地规划出来，好让它能围上一圈栅栏，好让聚会所成为欧洲的钟楼或城堡、或古典时代高高的庙址那样的集合中心。但到了十八世纪，印第安人在殖民地生活的格局里已无足轻重，沿海和沿河一带——如缅因州的韦尔斯海滩（Wells Beach）或康涅狄格的利奇菲尔德（Litchfield）——村庄变成大路上的一条长带，耕地从宅屋到水边以窄条伸展，好让农夫更周全地保护他的庄稼和牲畜，防备狐狸、狼、土拨鼠、鹰、臭鼬和鹿。

我强调这些结构上的要点，是因为肤浅的观察者从欧洲或新英格兰的村庄里，有时会带回一个愚蠢的观念：它们的不规则纯然出于任性、不讲经济，只与乱走的母牛的性子相关。更正确的说法恐怕恰恰相反。地块在大小与形状上的参差，表明人们始终注意土地所要履行的功能，而非单纯的财产占有。因此，坐落在村内的宅地与通常位于边缘的纯农业用地之间，大小便有所不同；比如在戴德姆，已婚男子分得十二英亩的宅地，而单身汉只有八英亩。村庄之所以紧凑，还有一个原因：1635 年马萨诸塞的总议会（General Court）敕令，任何新垦殖区里的住宅都不得距聚会所半英里以上。就连那些无法归因于这些明显考虑的房屋布局与位置上的不规则，也往往出自遮挡风的路径、争取夏天的良好朝向、或者借一处景致的企图。

这些都是真正的共同体规划。它或许不叫这个名字，但它达到了实效。

### III / 三

===Original===
We have learned in recent years to appreciate the felicities of eighteenth-century colonial architecture, and even the earlier seventeenth-century style is now coming into its own, in the sense that it is being imitated by architects who have an eye for picturesque effects; but we lose our perspective altogether if we think that the charm of an old New England house can be recaptured by designing overhanging second stories or panelled interiors. The just design, the careful execution, the fine style that brings all the houses into harmony no matter how diverse the purposes they served﻿—for the farmhouse shares its characteristics with the mill, and the mill with the meetinghouse﻿—was the outcome of a common spirit, nourished by men who had divided the land fairly and who shared adversity and good fortune together. When the frame of the house is to be raised, a man’s neighbors will lend him a hand; if the harvest is in danger, every man goes out into the fields, even if his own crop is not at stake; if a whale founders on the beach, even the smallest boy bears a hand, and gets a share of the reward. All these practices were not without their subtle effect upon craftsmanship.

Schooled in the traditions of his guild, the medieval carpenter pours his all into the work. Since sale does not enter into the bargain, it is both to his patron’s advantage to give him the best materials, and to his own advantage to make the most of them. If at first, in the haste of settlement, the colonists are content with makeshifts, they are nevertheless done in the traditional fashion﻿—not the log cabins of later days, but, more probably, wattle and daub huts like those of the charcoal burners in the English forests. In some points, the prevailing English tradition does not fit the raw climate of the north, and presently the half-timbered houses of some of the earlier settlers would be covered by clapboards for greater warmth, as in the eighteenth century their interiors were lined with panelled pine or oak, instead of the rough plaster. No matter what the material or mode, the carpenter works not simply for hire, but for dear life’s sake, and as a baker’s dozen numbers thirteen, so a piece of handicraft contains not merely the workmanship itself, but a bit of the worker’s soul, for good measure. The new invention of the gambrel roof, which gave additional room to the second story without raising the rooftree, is a product of this system; and the variation in its length and pitch in New England, New Jersey, and New York is a witness to the freedom of design that prevailed throughout the work.

These seventeenth-century houses, built at first with one or two rooms, and then as luxury increased and family needs multiplied with as many as four, would doubtless seem unspeakably crude and mean to the resident of Floral Heights; indeed, if our present requirements for housing were so simple it would not be quite so difficult to meet our perpetual shortage. As a matter of fact, however, these early provincial houses were well up to the standards for a similar homestead in England; and in some ways were a distinct advance.
